@@13770853 <p> The Indian Express Deputy Editor Seema Chishti with Amitav Ghosh at the Express Adda in Delhi . <p> Writer Amitav Ghosh , whose new book , The Great Derangement : Climate Change and the Unthinkable , deals with a global crisis , was a guest at the latest edition of the Express Adda held at The Lalit hotel in Delhi . He spoke to Deputy Editor , The Indian Express , Seema Chishti about climate change and its human costs as well as the dangers of living in denial of the problem . <p> On The Great Derangement <p> The crisis of climate change is not in the future , it is upon us . We can see it everywhere . Just look around -- the heat wave we had in Delhi ; the terrible deluge we had in Chennai last year . Impacts are multiplying around us . It 's here and now . Dealing with it ? Well , I 'm sure we have lots of experts in the room and I think it 's best that we leave it to the experts to decide @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ me is how I deal with it as a writer . That is , to me , the real challenge . In writing this book , I realised that what we all bring to a challenge of this kind is a bourgeois sensibility . That 's where we falter and it becomes impossible for us to address the issues that arise out of climate change . Experts can tell us how to fiddle with edges and adjust here and there but , I think , what climate change has laid bare for us is an enormous crisis of imagination . It 's a crisis of writing . <p> On religion and climate change <p> One of the world 's great climate change scientists is Prof V Ramanathan . He 's at the Scripps Institution of Oceanography in California . He is on the Pope 's advisory council and he makes the argument that the most hopeful thing is the Pope 's encyclical ( a letter sent by the Pope to bishops of the Roman Catholic Church ) . When I read the encyclical , I felt that it was the most @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It 's the most humane , most reasonable , most down-to-earth thing that I 've ever seen on climate change . When the Pope puts out a document like that , it reaches 1.2 billion people . As for other religious groups , there are some small Hindu groups , which have expressed an interest in climate change , as have some small Muslim groups . The Dalai Lama has spoken about climate change . But , I 'm afraid when you look around India and much of the Middle-East , you 'll find religious groups completely fixated with consumerism . Religion has become so complicit with industry and consumerism that it 's very hard to disassociate it . Erdogan 's version of Islam in Turkey is oriented to market friendliness . <p> Ravinder Kaur and Surjit S Bhalla , columnists , The Indian Express <p> On politics and business <p> The philosopher Martin Heidegger predicted something almost 70 or 80 years ago -- what he called global technicity is something that is totally out of control . It controls us . No one can stop this machine . Heidegger also @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that 's what we 're seeing today . <p> Everywhere in the world you look , what do politicians talk about ? It 's growth , growth , growth . It 's not like they can address anything else . In fact , the entire model of running a polity now is borrowed from industry . They speak of themselves and conceive of themselves as CEOs . Can you be really surprised that Donald Trump has made such a splash ? He 's actually a businessman and CEO . I can predict with absolute confidence that we are going to see that in India . I believe that some mega-billionaire will suddenly stand up and say , ' OK , I can run this better than the political class ' . I believe that lies in the future . <p> Amitav Ghosh with a memento presented by The Indian Express <p> On stories about migration <p> My interest in migration really began with my book , The Glass Palace . That touched on Indian indentured workers in Burma but I could n't go very deeply into it because the book is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ went back to the subject and I had an immediate connection with it . In my own family , there 's this whole history of displacement . My ancestors left Bangladesh in the 1850s because of a climate event ; a river shifted course and our village was drowned . They finally settled in Chhapra in Bihar . I grew up hearing Bhojpuri around me . My father 's first language was Bhojpuri . As I got deeper and deeper into the story , it spoke to me powerfully . The other experience that went into the Ibis Trilogy is that I left India to go to Oxford in 1978 , and how wrenching it was . I started to think what would it have been like for a woman from rural Bihar to suddenly find herself being uprooted to a place which she 's been told is full of demons . It 's Lanka , it 's Mareech , they 're going among the demons . Look how brave they were . I think they were heroes to embark on such a journey at that time -- throwing themselves into @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It was the girmitiyas on one hand and , on the other , I got interested in laskars , who were the 18th and 19th century sailors , who kept the merchant marine alive those days . One wonders how they felt to be out on the sea ; we know so little of their lives . Yet , they were the forerunners of today 's IT professionals . They were the first Indians to live in close contact with Europeans on the same ships . They were the first Indians to undergo industrial modes of discipline , such as the four-hour shifts . That was very difficult -- you sleep four hours , then you 're on duty four hours , and so on . It 's a very rigorous industrial discipline . <p> One of the important aspects of the bourgeois view of the world is a kind of sentimentalism whereby we always think of the world as getting better . These rules come from Hegel , where we think of the world spirit and , now , people are realising it 's not going to work out that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ realise will be that many of the problems that now beset us have no solution . They are not going to get better . The collapse of Syria started with a drought , which , in fact , is affecting the whole of the Mediterranean region . With the drought , you had hundreds of thousands of farmers abandoning their land and moving to the cities . This whole state began to collapse . There are several other reasons of course , but this is one of them . I read that , during the recent drought , lakhs of people moved from Budelkhand to Delhi every week . We may not see them or ignore them , they may live under flyovers , but there is going to be a long-term cost . This process of migration is deeply destabilising . After Syria , we 're seeing a similar scene in Turkey , and the Turkey story is far from over . We can see how it has destabilised Europe . There is this huge flood of migrants entering the Continent . <p> It is important for me as a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ keep some distance between myself and the spectacle that is unfolding around me . Guy Debord , a French intellectual , wrote a book , called The Society of Spectacle , in the ' 60s . It 's incredibly relevant to the world that we live in . What we have set in motion is a society of spectacle where we are all dragged into a continuous tamasha , a sort of spectacle in which we continuously participate . <p> It 's not that writers and artistes should not think about public affairs but they should think about what is important and long-term and not things that happen every day . That is what brought me to climate change . I see climate change unfolding all around us . <p> A packed house listens to the writer at the Express Adda <p> Writers , artistes and intellectuals are more political than ever before . They are political on so many issues , whether it is gender or personal freedoms and yet this wider thing that is unfolding around us and which will impact all our lives in every possible way , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It seems to me , this really is the thing that I want to address , that I want to think about , what are these long-term challenges that they present to the imagination . <p> VOICE BANK <p> Jaya JaitlyFormer president , Samata party <p> I come from a very old generation where we read Rachel Carson 's Silent Spring . I do n't think it is possible to efficiently manage these numbers . I do n't think it can be done logistically . I think more and more cities in India are becoming mega cities and I do n't think that can be turned back . <p> Radha SinghNon-executive part-time chairperson , YES Bank <p> I read your book last night and could n't sleep , out of guilt and from fear . Are you arguing against growth per se or the kind of growth @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ something wrong to pursue , or is it the manner in which it is being pursued that really irks us and is calling upon us to think of more serious issues like climate change ? <p> I think the problem is not growth , it is the way we conceive of growth , within certain really narrowly defined metrics . What is meaningful , what matters , the friends , family , community . In India , forest land can be diverted for non-forestry purposes , as per the provisions of the FC Act . <p> According to data from the Union ministry of environment , forest and climate change ( MoEFCC ) , a total of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ purposes like mining , power projects , road and other industrial projects between October 1980 and July 2016 . <p> If forests diverted for defence purposes ( 43,492 hectares ) and road sector ( 43,590 ) are added to the top three sectors , then they together account for nearly 46% ( 412,676 hectares ) of the total forest area diverted . <p> Among the states , Madhya Pradesh topped the list , with a total of 245,978 hectares of forest area diverted . The five states together account for over 57% ( 517,789 hectares ) of total forest land diverted since 1980 . <p> In reply to a query in the Lok Sabha on Tuesday , Union environment minister Anil Madhav Dave maintained that the diversion of forest area has not caused any ecological imbalance . <p> " The forest land diverted has not caused ecological imbalance . Since the enactment of the Forest ( Conservation ) Act , 1980 , the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on average approximately 25,000 hectares per year , " said Dave . <p> But he conceded that non-forest activities , including mining , which is the single biggest reason for the diversion of forest land , invariably have an impact on environment and ecological balance " . <p> " Deforestation for mining can lead to soil erosion , and water pollution , impact flora and fauna , " he explained . <p> The environment minister further stated that mitigative measures are mandated with every clearance granted like raising of compensatory afforestation , minimum tree felling , arrangement of alternate fuels , implementation of wildlife conservation plan ( wherever required ) , phased reclamation of mined out area and demarcation of boundary of mining lease , among others . <p> " Further , the regular monitoring of forest areas , after approval by the Central government for carrying out mining activities , is conducted by the state governments and regional office of the ( environment ) ministry to ensure that no violations occur and conditions mandated in the approval are complied with , " Dave explained .

@@13802655 <h> Kaphal flowers long after spring , experts blame climate change <p> Vineet Upadhyay <p> : The kaphal or box myrtle , a fruit which is culturally significant for communities across the Himalayas , including Uttarakhand , has been exhibiting unusual fruiting and ripening behaviour in Ramgarh , Nainital district . The fruit which should have ripened by April is doing so only now in August , a situation which environmentalists said points to climate change . <p> TNN Aug 12 , 2016 , 10.14 PM IST <p> NAINITAL : The kaphal or box myrtle , a fruit which is culturally significant for communities across the Himalayas , including Uttarakhand , has been exhibiting unusual fruiting and ripening behaviour in Ramgarh , Nainital district . The fruit which should have ripened by April is doing so only now in August , a situation which environmentalists said points to climate change . <p> A team from Centre for Ecology Development and Research ( CEDAR ) , Dehradun , visited some of the sites where the plant grows in abundance and marked areas for further monitoring and research . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , germination and so on is influenced by seasonal and climatic change . As plants and trees are tuned and susceptible to seasonality , any alteration in flowering or fruiting indicates that ecosystems are being influenced by changing climate , " said Vishal Singh , forest ecologist with CEDAR . <p> Phenological behaviour is the scientific study of periodic biological phenomena , such as flowering , breeding and migration , in relation to climatic conditions . Changes in climate may have a significant change in ' phenophase ' which includes bud formation , bud bursting , leafing , flowering , fruiting and ultimately leaf fall in plants . <p> " It is difficult to say anything concrete without long-term observations but the cycle has been disturbed for sure . A few explanations that I can provide for this unusual phenomenon include low winter rainfall in 2016 , resulting in moisture stress , " added Singh . <p> A similar phenomenon was observed by ecologists from Kumaon University in 1999 when Banj Oak , an oak tree , withheld springtime leaf production and produced leaves in several steps due to what was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the pattern clearly indicates that the cycle of fruiting has been affected . Changes in climate are definitely a factor , as this year there was negligible rainfall in winter . Other than this , we need to understand long-term reasons and effects of such changes , which are definitely not a good sign , " said Bhaskar Vira , director of Conservation Research Institute , University of Cambridge , who was in Nainital for research on urbanisation and its impact . <p> The kaphal 's seasonal nature is the subject of many popular folk songs across the Himalayas , with most mentioning it as a symbol of spring . ' Bedu Pako ' , a famous folk song of Uttarakhand , goes thus : " Bedu pako baro maasa , kaphal pako chaita " , which can be translated as " The bedu ( berries ) ripen around the year , but the kaphal ripens in Chaita ( mid-March to mid-April by the Hindu calendar ) " . <p> " The changed cycle threatens not only ecology but also local culture and economy . ' Aranya Sanskriti ' or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be lost if such trends continue , " said historian and activist Ajay Singh Rawat . <p> Stay updated on the go with Times of India News App . @@13804154 <p> For more than half a century , Noam Chomsky has been America 's pre-eminent dissenter . Ever since 1967 , when he wrote the essay Responsibilities of the Intellectual at the height of the protests in the United States against the Vietnam War , Chomsky has been never shirked from questioning the state . <p> This collection of articles is a good introduction to Chomsky for a generation whose exposure to critical intellectual discussion is confined to Twitter 's 140 characters and sound bites by " experts " on TV . Chomsky draws on history , going back even to the Magna Carta to expose what he sees as the deceit and lies of liberal democracy . If there is a basic approach underlying his arguments , it is to strip us of " historical amnesia " , which " is a dangerous phenomenon not only because it undermines moral and intellectual integrity but also because it lays the ground work for crimes that still lie ahead " ( p 43 ) . <p> The method of making the reader shake off amnesia is familiar Chomsky : @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and thereby show a difference in state response . One example : The world was horrified with a missile downing the Malaysian Airlines MH17 over the Ukraine in 2014 , which resulted in the death of 298 people ; the US , in particular , expressed outrage at alleged Russian complicity . But where was the outrage in 1980 , when the Vincennes , a US guided missile cruiser , shot down Iran Air 665 , a civilian aircraft operating in Iranian air space , with 290 people on board ? There was no US government outrage at the time , with the commander of the Vincennes being recognised two years later , we are reminded , for " exceptionally meritorious conduct " . Some would contest the use of examples of " equivalence " ( or the lack of it ) across time and contexts to make a point . But in a world where some states exercise much greater power than others , there is an unequal influence states have on public opinion , and such examples do bring out the imbalance in power . <p> The global issues @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ US involvement in central and south America to Palestine and Israel , and , of course , 9/11 and thereafter . In just three pages ( pp 249-51 ) , he lays bare the US role in destroying Afghanistan , Iraq and Libya , resulting in , among other things , the emergence of ISIS . The realists , who today dominate the world of commentators on security , would dismiss what they will see as Chomsky 's unidimensional critique of the US . But you need a Chomsky to clear the fog and cut to the core . <p> We can complain that for a book published in 2016 , there is not much of a discussion of the ISIS . The one problem is that the volume appears to be a collection of articles published elsewhere prior to 2015 , with only a few pieces being apparently updated or freshly written . And because of that , there is the occasional repetition across chapters . <p> Chomsky does not answer the question , " Who rules the world ? " Indeed , that there is no single entity ruling @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is described as the biggest terrorist state in the world . ( For those who argue that US power is on the decline , Chomsky acknowledges the relative decline in US economic dominance , but correctly insists that American military dominance continues . ) There is also the point that it is no longer only states that exercise extreme power , so do global financial institutions and multinational corporations . At one point , Chomsky approvingly quotes a commentator describing global people 's movements as constituting the " Second Superpower " . The argument -- based on strength of the global anti-Iraq War protests of 2003 -- is not developed . ) <p> It is only Chomsky who can make the fundamental point that when we speak of " security " , we should discuss the security of people , not states . A constant refrain in the collection it is that the world is on the edge of two forms of annihilation -- by nuclear weapons and climate change . A number of frightening ( and not so well-known ) events that Chomsky recounts from the time of the Cuban @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world has been to escape complete destruction . The end of the Cold War has not reduced that danger . Climate change ( discussed in relatively less detail ) is the second danger that we refuse to acknowledge , the threat being much greater than acknowledged by official bodies . Both these extreme dangers tend to be ignored in most discussions of security . <p> An enormous amount of reading of both primary and secondary sources underlies Chomsky 's writings . The reader will yet find that the collection is not cluttered with footnotes ; instead the end notes at the back of the book document the sources of many insights and gems . <p> Here are just two . In 1952 , Stalin made a proposal that Germany could become one and elections be held in the unified country , provided the new Germany did not join a military alliance . The US rejected the proposal ( p182 ) . What may have been the consequences for world peace if the US had followed up on the Soviet proposal ? The second one in these days of Islamophobia is of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ US , saying that the newly independent US should be concerned about Germans entering the country because " they were too swarthy " . The American icons were not free from racism .

@@13812686 <p> A profound respect for Irom Sharmila 's decision is need of the hour 13 August , 2016 <p> Irom Sharmila 's decision to withdraw her fast has come as a blow in varying degrees to many . There are also many others who welcome her decision to end her fast instead of sticking on to it at ridiculous levels . I am thinking of the many NGO leaders who invested their energy and money on Irom with the hope that their credibility will soar high at the international arena . <p> They projected her as their prodigy and as an icon promoted by them . They celebrated their income that flowed through the dreamy castles they constructed around the plummeting image of Irom Sharmila and now all of a sudden they find their castles crumbling like ninepins under their feet . Will Sharmila resurrect herself to serve their purposes ? Will they resurrect Irom to camouflage their inevitable defeat ? <p> Family members and activist friends of Irom have started shunning her immediately after she ended her fast . We can not steer clear of the inexorable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ espoused , why are they shunning her now ? Was she a pawn in their hands , I mean , did they expect her to be a pawn ? " There was a long time when she believed that her fast would lead to the lifting of AFSPA . <p> Now she realises that her calculations did not , do not and will not pay in the way that she and her friends anticipated . She must be appreciated for the courage she has manifested in the withdrawal of the fast . Does anyone think that she did not foresee the type of knottiness that her withdrawal of the fast would heap on her ? A profound respect for her decision , however , unpalatable it might be for many , is the dire need of the hour . <p> Irom Sharmila can quickly turn the table on her ' promoters ' and supporters and say that she was allowed to be imprisoned in the hospital for more than 16 long years . The civil society that supported her cause failed miserably and that she is left with no other option @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so far , as far as I know . The civil society had much more space for maneuvering and more power to lobby . She can easily conclude that her incarceration in the hospital served the agenda of the civil society much better than her espoused cause of freedom for the people of Manipur . <p> There is a need to understand the fate of resistance in a strongly evolving capitalistic nation state . It will take recourse to Plato , Chanakya , Machiavelli , and Nietzsche , without ever mentioning their names , to put down resistance at all costs . The postmodern capitalist state has wholesomely outsmarted all these greats of all time in strategising its march into the formative elements of the state as its agent . <p> Unfortunately , the civil society has no idea of the enormous power the state can wield wildly at anything that is remotely perceived as anti-state . The civil society takes on the state rhetorically and not strategically . It has no patience to study the deep rooted ' evil ' forethought of the state and its undeclared strategies not only @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the same under attractive packages for the needy . Some leaders can be mesmerizing in their counter rhetoric pulling away the masses to their sides . <p> Thus , resistance often assumes the face of reaction to something that the state has already implemented . It is a paramount duty of the resistant forces to gauge the internal sustainability of their resistance for a long duration . In the absence of such objective assessment , the state will be laughing at itself and counter gauging the stretch of resistance . <p> Civil society does not have the grasp of issues , the patience and the wherewithal to proactively initiate and sustain the life struggles of the citizens . It does not have free access to material resources for the sustainability of resistance as the state has for the sustainability of aggression and exploitation . It is the simple history . Many resistance movements achieved their victory because they were able to point physically out the oppressor or the enemy to the people and converge their energy resource for the fight . <p> The civil society is often seen at the crossroads @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ when it comes to identifying the real ' adversary . ' Often there is no such opposing side that the people can see . When the civil society points out to the state as the other side , the voters see it as the same side with them . It seems to be the crux of the problem , that the people elect the governments and the civil society points its fingers to the governments as anti-people . The laborious point of the intellectual civil society is that the majority of voters did n't vote for a particular government . Well said ! <p> What has the civil society done to change the equation ? It does not realize that there are electoral systems that will provide representation to all voters ? It is not even aware that there are other systems of elections to make representation worthy of democracy , people 's power . The world today has already seen 89 countries shifting to proportional representation system to make their democracy functional in favour of the voters . <p> The Indian civil society is still hobnobbing with the British system @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ On this front , both the governments and the civil society are hand in hand . A representative electoral system that will favour the Adijans ( Dalits ) , Adivasis , minorities and women is seen as an enemy by the caste society . <p> When they say that governments are anti-people , and they join hands with the governments to support an electoral system that benefits the same government , there is a problem of credibility of the civil society . The dividing line between the caste society and civil society in India seems to be quite diluted in mesmerizing discourses of democracy and governance . <p> The inevitable conclusion then is that like the many other agencies that support governments , whom they project as anti-people , civil society is also a tacit supporter of the rulers . Their anti-people slogan can have only a rhetoric value . The same capitalistic and caste values seem to be the underlying bond between the governments and civil society in India . <p> Such a civil society will not have the teeth to go in solidarity with the likes of Irom Sharmila @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ AFSPA than the civil society that is angry at her now . <p> Editorial NOTE : This article is categorized under Opinion Section . The views expressed in this article are solely those of the author and do not necessarily represent the views of merinews.com . In case you have a opposing view , please click here to share the same in the comments section . <p> About The Author <p> M C Raj is a human rights activist and award-winning author of more than 18 books . He has initiated a national campaign for proportionate electoral system , popularly known as CERI , in India . He is deeply involved in Climate Change issues . 
@@13816633 <h> To counter the influx of beetles owing to climate change , farmers of Majuli island have been encouraged to catch the insect and eat it . <p> The farmers of Majuli , a riverine island on the Brahmaputra River in Assam , have turned the beetles that attacked their crops into their newest cuisine . Majuli , one of the largest fresh water islands in the world , has been severely infested by the beetle lepidiota mausuet , locally known as the hati-puk , since 2005 . It has taken a severe toll on agriculture . <p> The island has a crop area of over 30,000 hectares , and up to 70% of this is attacked by this pest . Now the farmers , with support from the Assam Agricultural University , catch the pest before they damage the crops and eat them ! <h> Beetle boom <p> According to scientists at the Assam Agricultural University , although the insect was always present on the island , it became a menace only after 2005 . They blame climate change for this infestation . <p> Research undertaken by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sugarcane and green gram cultivation . As a result , thousands of farmers have been affected . <p> Badal Bhattacharyya advising a farmer on how to deal with the beetle image courtesy AINSAP <p> " The insect is found in such large numbers only in Majuli , probably because of the soil content and availability of water , " said Badal Bhattacharyya , senior scientist and principal investigator at the Department of Entomology of Assam Agricultural University . <p> According to Bhattacharyya , increase in temperature , erratic rainfall and early onset of summer has led to an increase in the beetle population . At the same time , the migratory Siberian crane ( Grus leucogeranus ) , which is a major predator of the pest , has stopped wintering there . <p> " The Siberian crane used to ensure that the beetle is kept in check , but nowadays , the bird rarely comes here , " he said . <h> A new cuisine <p> However , now the people do n't need the Siberian crane to catch the beetle , as they have developed delicious beetle dishes , which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and his research team have been popularising the dish with the slogan , " Eat it before it eats your crops . " <p> On being asked if the beetle was safe to eat , he explained that at the university , research was carried out on the beetle , and it was found that it is rich in protein and carbohydrates , there is no toxic content and the insect could be nutritional food for humans and poultry . <p> The many dishes made from the beetle : roasted beetle fry with tomato , plain roasted beetle and beetle curry have gained popularity . According to locals , it tastes like prawn . <p> " We started these dishes here three years back and it has been a huge hit , and became popular instantly . Apart from local people , even tourists have tasted the dishes and have liked those , " said Milanjyoti Kuli , a farmer from the Kuli-chapori village in Majuli who is taking steps to popularise these dishes . <h> Dining at the pestaurant <p> Kuli , with support from the Assam Agricultural University , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ dishes for locals and tourists . Kuli said the dishes have not been priced yet , and the mission at present is just to popularise these dishes . <p> Advertising the Pestaurant image courtesy AINSAP <p> " The results have been much better than our expectations and very soon , we plan to start a few restaurants that will sell these dishes , " said Kuli . " We are confident that it will be a huge success as Majuli is a major tourism destination for both domestic and foreign tourists . " <p> He said the university has already launched a campaign to popularise the dishes all across the island . <p> Locals pointed out that there is an opportunity for entrepreneurship as well , since the dishes are universally popular , and safe to eat . " We will start a restaurant next year on the island , and if it yields good results , we might plan to launch the dishes outside Majuli as well , " said Bhubon Bora , a local youth from the Kamalabari village in Majuli . Bora added that the basic raw material @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , so it is a venture worth undertaking . <h> Going for the grub <p> The real target , though , is not the mature beetle but its grub ( larva ) . " The white grub lives in the soil for nearly two years before reaching maturity , all the while gnawing at the roots of crop and causing widespread damage , " said Bhattacharyya . " The adult beetle does not feed on any plant , so the concern is the grub . " <p> He pointed out that the ideal time to catch beetles and to reduce their population is between April and June , when they come out to mate . After that , they burrow deep underground and produce 30-35 eggs in one batch . At these depths , insecticides do not reach them . <p> To deal with the situation , villagers have been mobilised to catch the beetles when they come out to mate . Farmers groups are formed , with 10 members each , and they have played a major role in spreading information on how to deal with the menace . There @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them by shining light on the fields as they are attracted to light , and in these two months thousands of beetles are caught , " said Bhattacharyya . <p> The initiative started in 2012 through mass campaigning , and between April to June that year over 43,000 beetles were caught . " The beetles are caught using solar LED light traps , and are placed in both cultivated and non-cultivated areas , " he said . This year the farmers had caught 170,000 thousand beetles . <p> Farmers had suffered to the point of giving up , but this beetle catching has changed their prospects . Jibon Payeng , another farmer from the Kuli-Chapori village , said that in 2011 he had almost made up his mind to look for other earning options . <p> " However after 2012 things seem to have returned to normalcy , and the pest attacks have reduced greatly , " said Payeng . " We are hopeful that within the next few years , things will further improve . " <h> Young India is leading life its own way , changing the rules their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ never easy . It takes a lot of self-belief and perseverance to carve your own path . In this age , when ' being yourself ' is an over-used concept , it is easy to mistake it for superficial rebelliousness . But truly living your life your way goes beyond that to actually following your passion , pushing yourself even in the absence of role models or societal encouragement and letting your own fire fuel your potential . These individuals had a different vision for themselves , and chose to follow that : <p> Priyanka Chopra . Actress . Singer . International star . Priyanka Chopra does n't fit any single description . She has taken her career from beauty pageants to music videos , Bollywood movies and international television shows . Her most recent adventure has seen her turn producer of regional language films . After returning from the US , Rakyan observed the lack of natural health drink options and decided to enter a market that was predominantly filled with processed juices . Even in the fresh juices space , few knew of and appreciated the concept of cold-pressed juices . It was a bold step , but Anuj remained convinced it would work . Today , Raw Pressery is proving successful with two rounds of investment from Sequioa Capital . <p> Anuj Rakyan <p> Zumba Queen -- Sucheta Pal . One day , she decided to give up her job and become a dancer full-time . She struggled with societal norms since dancing was n't even seen as a serious career option @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ dance fitness program , in the US and has n't looked back ever since . Pal is the lead Master Trainer for India and is certified as a Zumba Education Specialist by Zumba Fitness . She has trained over 3,000 trainers and is credited with popularizing Zumba in the country . <p> Sucheta Pal <p> TV Tough Guy -- Rannvijay Singh . @@13817968 <p> Regimes change every few years , the rupee is dropping , and climate change is heating up our seas , but the one great , immutable truth about life is that nowhere in the universe does time move slower than in a Sri Lankan government office . <p> The island 's bureaucrats are a marvel of evolution . Having come to life in a primordial soup of bribes , backhanders and political favours , these folk have , over billions of years , become perfectly adapted to moving at speeds that make sea cucumbers look like rocket ships , to answering questions with statements of peerless ambiguity , and to being exquisitely adept at maximising the frustration of every non-bureaucrat in a few-kilometres radius . <p> The SSC ground happens to be in the clotted heart of Colombo 's administrative district . Yet , although the Australia team may never set foot inside the National Identity Card Centre , which is a few minutes away , or the Ministry of Public Administration and Management , which is visible from the ground , they were on day one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ They were made to wait for hours , run around , given brief hope , which was promptly crushed ; they were sneered at , and mistreated , and by the end of the day , they were ready to tear their hair out . <p> Bureaucrat-in-chief for Sri Lanka was Dhananjaya de Silva , whose maiden Test hundred was a thing of superlative casualness . So unfazed was he by a scoreline of 26 for 5 at his arrival , he was like the civil servant who comes to work to find a queue stretching around the building outside his office , yet before work takes a long tea-and-biscuit break while chit-chatting to colleagues . He stood slouched at the crease all day , unfurling somnolent drives , and languid flicks to the leg side , appearing as if he was doing almost nothing . Meanwhile , Australia chased leather all over ; patrons shunted from room to room to room in the building , all , in the end , for no tangible reward . <p> Several times , the visitors thought they had him . He was given @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ faced , but that decision was overturned . Twice , when he was in the forties , Australia were so convinced he was out they chanced their own reviews , but had them turned down . On 73 , his outside edge was not snared by Peter Nevill . On 104 , he was dropped at short cover by Shaun Marsh . Patrons at a government office know what it is like to fill out the same form over and over , and after hopefully taking it to the desk , being told that the words must be written in black ink , not blue , or that a different stamp is required , or that the office is shutting early , so the date is wrong and the whole thing must be tried again another day . <p> So unfazed was Dhananjaya de Silva by a scoreline of 26 for 5 at his arrival , he was like the civil servant who comes to work to find a queue stretching around the building outside his office , yet before work takes a long tea-and-biscuit break while chit-chatting to colleagues . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that he began to hurl the ball at de Silva 's stumps , in his follow-through , though there was little chance of running the batsman out . De Silva , still slouching , had the expression of someone who sees this kind of thing all the time . So he sent in Starc 's direction a blank , condescending stare : " If you make a fuss , it will only make things more difficult for you . " <p> Dinesh Chandimal was by far the more experienced hand in their partnership , which suggests Chandimal should be the more efficient of the two . But , just as in bureaucracy , the opposite was true . Chandimal scored 64 from 204 balls - a strike rate of 31 , where de Silva 's was 48 . Having been embedded in this institution for several years now , Chandimal has less to prove . Already having risen to vice-captain of the team , incentives are slim . He was even more excited about his partner 's century , than de Silva was himself - possibly because every run de Silva @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Lanka are far from impregnable in this match , but De Silva 's insouciance has delivered them a strong position . The pitch is not spitting yet , but it is already taking so much turn that Jon Holland was seen in dustier and dustier whites through the day , in what may be a brazen attempt to take the pitch home with him . <p> And Australia are also yet to face the slow , loopy slider , which is the ball that in Sri Lanka has wreaked havoc upon them in a manner that brings to mind Godzilla 's destruction of a Tokyo city block , or a standard Sri Lanka Cricket official 's effect on Sri Lankan cricket . <p> For large parts of Saturday , Australia found themselves getting absolutely nowhere . @@13824322 <h> TISS Guwahati plans to promote NE crafts <p> GUWAHATI : The Guwahati campus of Tata Institute of Social Sciences ( TISS ) will soon promote traditional crafts of the northeast by venturing into the skill development sector . <p> The deputy director of TISS 's Guwahati campus , DK Srivastava , said the institute was exploring the possibility of joining hands with National Skill Development Corporation ( NSDC ) , National Skill Development Agency ( NSDA ) and the Assam government to promote traditional crafts of the region . <p> On the sidelines of a conclave aimed at ' Engaging Youth in Sustainable Development Goals ' here on Saturday , Srivastava said , " We have been successful in promoting the handloom sector in Uttar Pradesh , especially chikankari ( traditional embroidery work of Lucknow ) and Varanasi silk . We want to do something similar in the northeast . " <p> At the conclave , which was organized by TISS and InspireX Centre for Sustainable Development , Srivastava said youngsters should be engaged through innovative methods and they should be made part of every development goal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and universities , and members of youth clubs of the region took part in the daylong conclave which discussed the United Nation 's sustainable development goals ( SDGs ) to fight poverty , inequality , injustice and climate change by 2030 . The goals were adopted at a UN summit on sustainable development on September 25 , 2015 . <p> TISS assistant professor Shalini Sharma said the SDGs are ' aspirational ' and takes into account the realities of individual countries and the geo-political situations . " We must engage with these aspirational ideas . There has to be institutional means of fulfilling the aspirations of youths , " she said . <p> Associate professor of the Omeo Kumar Das Institute of Social Change and Development , Joydeep Barua , said people often mistake the SDGs as being entirely concerned about the environment . " But SDGs have a social dimension , too . Sustainability is not possible without curbing inequalities in society , " he said . <p> Stay updated on the go with Times of India News App . @@13826523 <h> Climate change threatens population of primates : Study <p> Barbary macaque monkeys lounge at the ' Top of the Rock ' on June 23 , 2016 in Gibraltar . ( Getty Images - File Photo ) <p> Shares6 <p> Sun , 14 Aug 2016-12:37pm , Toronto , PTI <p> A new study shows that climate change may seriously affect the world 's primate populations . <p> Climate change is not only an increasing concern for humans but rising sea levels and climbing temperatures may also threaten our closest cousins - monkeys , apes and lemurs , a new study has found . <p> The study shows that the world 's primate populations may be seriously affected by climate change . <p> " Our research shows that climate change may be one of the biggest emerging threats to primates , compounding existing pressures from deforestation , hunting and the exotic pet trade , " said Tanya Graham from Concordia University in Canada . <p> Researchers assessed the exposure and potential vulnerability of all non-human primate species to projected future temperature and precipitation changes . <p> They found that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ various species of lemurs , lorises , tarsiers , monkeys and apes - will experience 10 percent more warming than the global average , with some primate species experiencing increases of more than 1.5 degrees Celsius in annual average temperature for every degree of global warming . <p> Researchers also identified several hotspots of primate vulnerability to climate change , based on the combination of the number of species , their endangered status and the severity of climate changes at each location . <p> Overall , the most extreme hotspots , which represent the upper 10 percent of all hotspot scores , cover a total area of 3,622,012 square kilometres over the ranges of 67 primate species , researchers said . <p> The highest hotspot scores occur in Central America , the Amazon and southeastern Brazil , as well as portions of East and Southeast Asia - prime territory for some of the globe 's best-known primates who call these areas home , they said . <p> The ursine howler monkey , black howler monkey , and barbary macaque are expected to be exposed to the highest magnitude of climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ example , the ursine howler monkey , found in Venezuela , will experience an increase of 1.2 degrees Celsius annually and a 5.3 percent decline in annual rainfall for each degree of global temperature increase . <p> " This study highlights the vulnerability of individual species , as well as regions in which primates as a whole may be vulnerable to climate change , " said Damon Matthews from Concordia . <p> " Our findings can be taken as priorities for ongoing conservation efforts , given that any success in decreasing other current human pressures on endangered species may also increase that species ' ability to withstand the growing pressures of climate changes , " said Tanya Graham from Concordia . <p> The findings were published in the International Journal of Primatology. 
@@13827779 <p> Fixing the Great Barrier Reef will cost Australia 8.2 billion dollars over the next 10 years , an Australian minister said . <p> Reuters <p> Queenland 's Environmental Minister , Steven Miles , confirmed that Australia will need eight times more than the initial one billion dollars set aside by the federal government to nurse the reefs back to health . <p> The Great Barrier Reef is the largest living structure in the world and the largest coral reef . It can be seen from space and unsurprisingly so because it is more than 2,300km in length and has almost 3,000 individual reef , covering an area about the size of Germany . <p> This natural wonder , unfortunately , has suffered large-scale bleaching where 93% of its individual reefs have suffered from it . The cause is , of course , environmental degradation and climate change . Until effects are put into gear to save earth 's oceans from pollution , the reefs can not be saved . Overshoot is among the issues in preparatory documents . <p> Developing nations see overshoot as a betrayal of commitments by the rich and a recipe to worsen heatwaves such as in the Middle East this year or a thaw of Greenland 's ice sheet that could swamp island states by raising global sea levels . <p> " There is a risk that ' overshoot ' is a slippery slope towards lower ambition , " said Emmanuel de Guzman , secretary of the Climate Commission of the Philippines , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Climate Vulnerable Forum ( CVF ) . <h> Olympics Campaign <p> Backing that view at the Rio Olympics , some athletes have signs saying : " 1.5 - the record we must not break " in a campaign partly run by the CVF , whose members includes Bangladesh , the Maldives and Guatemala . <p> Developing nations say overshoot lets world leaders pay lip service to 1.5C while failing to act on pledges made in Paris for a trillion-dollar shift from coal and other fossil fuels towards renewable energies . <p> U.S. Republican presidential candidate Donald Trump doubts climate change is caused by human activities and has said he will pull out of the Paris Agreement if elected . If applied at a wide enough scale , such " negative emissions " could reduce temperatures after an overshoot . But there are many pitfalls . <p> The simplest natural aid - planting trees that absorb carbon dioxide from the air to grow - would probably require too much farmland to be feasible . In the process , he created the kind of government neither he nor the Republicans wanted -- one that depended on bureaucratic bulldozing rather than legislative transparency . But once Mr. Obama got @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ without apology , and in ways that will shape the presidency for decades to come . <p> The Obama administration in its first seven years finalised 560 major regulations -- those classified by the Congressional Budget Office as having particularly significant economic or social impacts . <p> That was nearly 50 per cent more than the George W. Bush administration during the comparable period , according to data kept by the regulatory studies centre at George Washington University . <p> The administration 's regulatory legacy has become an issue in the campaign to replace Mr. Obama , as Donald Trump has sharply criticised regulatory overreach and promised to undo many of the new rules . But executive power has expanded steadily under both Republican and Democratic Presidents in recent decades , and both Mr. Trump and Hillary Clinton have promised to act in the service of their own goals . <p> The new rules built on the legislative victories Mr. Obama won during his first two years in office . Those laws -- the Affordable Care Act , the Dodd-Frank Act and the $800 billion economic stimulus package -- transformed the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the big banks and injected financial support into a creaky economy . But as Republicans increased their control of Capitol Hill , Mr. Obama 's deep frustration with congressional opposition led to a new approach : He gradually embraced a President 's power to act unilaterally . <p> A White House push to pass a sweeping climate change bill in 2009 failed in Congress , but almost from the outset , some of Mr. Obama 's aides were working on a Plan B. Cass R. Sunstein , Mr. Obama 's choice to lead the White House office that oversees rule-making , and Michael Greenstone , the first head of Mr. Obama 's Council of Economic Advisers , created an internal task force to put a dollar figure on the cost of carbon emissions . <p> In January 2014 , the President stood before Congress and declared " a year of action " -- with or without the help of the Republicans arrayed before him . -- New York Times News Service

@@13846628 <h> India is pioneering in making advanced ultra supercritical equipment for coal power plants <p> A pioneering indigenous technology for coal-fired power plants , which requires less fuel , delivers greater efficiency and is a tad cleaner , will be ready in four years -- possibly ahead of other countries in the race . Revealing this to IANS in an interview , Indira Gandhi Centre for Atomic Research ( IGCAR ) Director Arun Kumar Bhaduri said : " The research , development and design phases of the 800 MW advanced ultra supercritical ( AUSC ) boiler for coal power plant will be ready by 2019-2020 . " <p> The timeline for the construction of an actual 800 MW power plant with AUSC boiler would depend on the regulatory approvals for the plant design and the funding of the plant construction , he added . The Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs , chaired by Prime Minister Narendra Modi , has approved a proposal for the research and development of AUSC technology for thermal power plants at an estimated cost of Rs 1,554 crore ( over $230 million ) . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by the Department of Heavy Industry ( DHI ) -- of Rs 900 crore spread over three years , commencing from 2017-18 , to power equipment major Bharat Heavy Electricals Ltd ( BHEL ) to implement the R&amp;D project . There will be contributions of Rs 270 crore from BHEL , Rs 50 crore from power generating company NTPC Ltd , Rs 234 crore from IGCAR and Rs 100 crore from the Department of Science and Technology ( DST ) . <p> The Indian government has proposed a National Mission for the Development of Ultra Supercritical Technology for thermal power plants as the ninth such under the National Action Plan for Climate Change under the guidance of R. Chidambaram , the government 's Principal Scientific Adviser . As per plans , the IGCAR will develop the material for 800 MW AUSC boiler that can operate at a pressure of 310 bar ( a measure of steam pressure ) and at a temperature of 710 degrees centigrade . <p> Power equipment major BHEL will design and manufacture the boiler and other equipment while power generator NTPC will be the end user to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ funds Bhaduri said : " We have taken this challenging project and would complete the mission . " When asked about the 800 MW capacity instead of higher one like 1,000 MW , Bhaduri said : " The capacity was decided taking into account factors like cost to power ratio and others . " <p> According to him , once the first 800 MW AUSC boiler powered thermal power plant starts operations the capacity may be re-evaluated . He said NTPC will decide on the location of the 800 MW AUSC boiler power plant . It will be in one of the locations where NTPC has power plants powered by super critical boilers . According to Bhaduri , 10 pre-project activities were taken up for research on specific gap areas . <p> " We have now come to a stage where research and development for the finalisation of the design of the plant could be started from a higher platform , " he added . According to him , all the aspects of a power plant -- boiler , turbine and balance of plant -- have been taken into consideration . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ components for the plant , " he said . " We have optimised the usage of an existing one so that they perform well at high temperature . The boiler tubes tested have shown better properties than internationally available , " Bhaduri remarked . <p> The AUSC project will enable Indian industries to design , manufacture and commission higher-efficiency coal-fired power plants with indigenously developed technology and manufacturing processes . " This will be the first time that large power plant equipment will be manufactured with advanced technologies , but without any technological collaboration/licensing agreement with foreign companies , " the central government said . The proposed technology is still in research stage in all countries working on it . @@13855643 <p> Plan to replace forests over thousands of acres in Kodagu , Shivamogga and Chikkamagaluru districts with plantations is a ticking time bomb ; it will lead to reduced inflow into rivers that feed Bengaluru and Mysuru , say experts <p> It was predicted that , this year , the state would witness a bumper monsoon . Following years of drought , everyone was pinning their hopes on this year 's rains to wash away the state 's agrarian and drinking water woes . <p> However , to everyone 's surprise , this year 's monsoon has been dismal , at least as far as reservoirs and hydel power are concerned . Major reservoirs like KRS and Linganamakki are still far from brimming , and with only a couple of months of monsoon left , the rest of the year could well be arid . So what 's got wrong with the weather ? <p> The Western Ghats are catchment regions for two major rivers , Cauvery and Sharavathi : Both supply nearly half of the state 's population . The degradation of the state 's pristine forests @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The government was warned about the weak monsoon by scientists and conservationists , yet it chose to ignore these warnings . The situation is likely to take a turn for the worse in the coming months , cities like Bengaluru and Mysuru will be among the hardest hit as they are dependent on the rivers . <p> Rather than learning a lesson , plans are under way to destroy a further 70,000 acres of forest in Shivamogga and Chikkamagaluru districts to make way for monoculture plantations . <p> Scientists from the Indian Institute of Science ( IISc ) , and conservationists from Uttara Kannada , Shivamogga and Kodagu have been critical of the government 's decision . Backed by studies and experiments spanning several decades in the Malnad region , scientists have concluded that the mindless destruction of forests and the introduction of monoculture plantations like teak , acacia , and eucalyptus ; and horticulture plantations for timber and industrial purposes has resulted in poor inflow to major rivers like Cauvery and Sharavathi . <p> 21% forest lost <p> Just as the government is busy trying to analyse what has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have revealed that as much as 21 per cent of the natural forest has been lost in the region over the last 40 years . <p> Giving an insight into the trend , Dr TV Ramachandra , Centre for Ecological Science , IISc , told Bangalore Mirror that the destruction of natural forests has affected the hydrological system of the region . <p> " Our researchers have studied the land-use pattern , rainfall and destruction of forest cover in Uttara Kannada ( Sharavathi catchment ) and also the catchment area of the Lakshmana Theertha River ( a tributary of the Cauvery in Kodagu ) . Between 1973 and 2014 , the forest cover percentage drastically came down from 43 per cent to a mere 22 per cent . This apart , about 36,000 hectares of forest area has been encroached upon to make room for various activities that have disturbed the ecosystem . Several monoculture plantations have come up in the catchment areas of both rivers that have affected the water-retaining capacity of the soil in the region . " <p> Acknowledging that this year 's rainfall has been poor , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ last week that in June and July , the district has seen low rainfall . " Compared to previous years , we have received an average of 40 per cent less rainfall this year . This is indeed a concern for all of us as it will have a cascading effect on the supply of potable water and electricity generation . " <p> Huge variation in rainfall <p> Collecting data from as early as 1970 , the research team analysed the pattern of rainfall every year . <p> " The Linganamakki catchment is spread across 1,900 sq km in the Western Ghats . Before the construction of the dam , the area used to receive about 3,500 to 4,200mm of rainfall every year . However , after the construction of the dam , it is only receiving about 2,000mm rainfall . While the eastern side of the dam ( towards the sea ) continues to receive the same intensity of rainfall , the western side ( hinterland ) has seen a reduction in rainfall over the years . This is due to the change in land usage patterns , as huge @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rehabilitation and developmental works , " Ramachandra explained . <p> Similarly , in the Lakshmana Theertha basin , the unique shola forest and grassland ecosystem were damaged by monoculture plantations and power projects . " Wherever the shola forests and grasslands remained intact , the water flow was perennial and it was hit wherever the forests were damaged . Had we maintained the similar diversified forest cover , we would not have been complaining now . Perhaps , this is the reason that KRS in Mandya has not crossed the 100-feet mark this year despite normal rainfall , " Ramachandra revealed . <p> Interestingly , several people backed by political parties opposed Ramachandra and his team 's proposal to study rainfall and climate change effect in other parts of Kodagu . <p> Western Ghats bring rains to Karnataka <p> Revealing that the entire stretch of Western Ghats bring rains to Karnataka , Dr Ramachandra said , " Everybody knows that Western Ghats are a sensitive region . But nobody understands that the Western Ghats brings rain to Karnataka . Due to plant diversity , the soil of the region has water-holding @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Thus the entire region acts as a sponge and ensures water inflow into rivers , through the year . With rapid , large-scale deforestation and introduction of monoculture plantations in the area , the native forest diversity has been lost . <p> " Monoculture plantations like teak and acacia have resulted in the creation of a thick layer of leaves that prevents water from being absorbed into the soil . Further , their leaves take a long time to decompose compared to native trees , and these act as barriers for natural percolation activity . Due to this , water retention capacity in the region has come down , affecting the inflow . " <p> 70,000 acres of rich forestland to go <p> While a considerable percentage of forest has been lost to commercial and plantation activity , now another 70,000 acres of forests in Shivamogga and Chikkamagalur districts are likely to witness similar destruction , thus affecting lives of the urban population in the coming months . <p> The government 's inordinate delay in regenerating lost forests and safeguarding existing forest cover spread across 70,000 acres of plantation area dedicated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ worried . <p> Suggesting a similar type of water crisis in the near future , Ananth Hegde Ashisara , former chairman of Western Ghats Task Force , told Bangalore Mirror that there is absolutely no management of plantations in the region . <p> " About 500 hectares of plantation has remained barren over the last two years . Around 2,500 hectares of acacia and eucalyptus plantations are awaiting harvest . Nurseries in the region are ready with two lakh saplings , but there is hardly anybody to plant them , and all activities have stopped abruptly . @@13856308 <h> Bengaluru startup uses satellite data to help farmers <p> BENGALURU : Crop loss due to harsh climate change is the bitter reality for many Indian farmers . It will be more cost-efficient than using drones due to its large area coverage . My ultimate aim is to work with the Karnataka government , this is my way of giving it back to my state . We are planning to meet agriculture minister Krishna Byregowda . " <p> Initially , the team had thought of a sponsored space-driven project . <p> Prateep Basu , analyst , Northern Sky Research who has been advising SatSure says , " There is so much data in space that we can make use of it to assess the health and yield of the crop and plan better . <p> " In India , insurance officers have to visit farms to verify crop loss . @@13858559 <h> Amaravati , an Example of Flawed Urban Policy <h> Share this : <h> Andhra Pradesh 's new ' world-class ' capital city will require enormous amounts of energy and resources to build and to run , while destroying precious natural environments and local livelihoods . <p> The planned Amaravati city . Credit : Wikimedia Commons <p> On August 10 , the National Green Tribunal heard a report raising objections to Amaravati , the new capital city of Andhra Pradesh planned by the state government -- the Chandrababu Naidu-led Telugu Desam Party . <p> Delhi-based architect Romi Khosla and physicist Vikram Soni , who have been working on their concept of the ' natural city ' since 2014 , say that their concept presents a new model for Asia 's urbanisation . They collaborated with Satyanarayana Bolisetti , an activist from Andhra Pradesh who has been fighting for farmers ' rights and the protection of river floodplains , on the report . It argues that Amaravati 's planned location on the southern floodplains of the river Krishna violates laws , making the city environmentally unsustainable and potentially dangerous @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an alternate Amaravati , or ' Amaravati Natural City ' , and have submitted these plans to Naidu 's office , which has not yet responded . <p> A global and narrow vision <p> Using a method called ' land pooling ' -- that has been criticised by farmers , activists and others -- the Naidu government has acquired over 30,000 acres of land on the southern banks of the river Krishna . It promises a ' world-class ' capital city in the style of Singapore or Dubai , with glittering corporate and government buildings overlooking the river , wide avenues , a golf course , high-rise residential complexes and malls and casinos on islands in the river . The Singapore-based firm Subarna Jurong prepared the master plan for Amaravati . <p> This particular vision is unsurprising and far from original . It is in line with the corporate-driven , trickle down model of development that international consultant organisations such as the McKinsey Global Institute and Scott Wilson have recommended India follow in the coming decades , and that the Narendra Modi government has adhered to . <p> Khosla has addressed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Awakening , in an article he wrote in Economic and Political Weekly . Writing about establishing industrial corridors , mega-cities and high-speed rail lines and roads , Khosla warns : <p> " The enormous footprint and mega presence of new projects does not address the critical factor of ecological balance through self-sufficiency in a global environment which is struggling to fight climate change . The implementation of these projects in the form planned in these reports will destroy precious natural environmental resources , while at the same time snatching what remains from existing users by impoverishing villages and farms . " <p> As Khosla , Soni and several other experts have been pointing out , mega-cities require enormous -- unsustainable -- amounts of energy and resources to build and to run , while destroying precious natural environments and local livelihoods . Amaravati promises to be but the next example of this kind of flawed urban policy . <p> The BJP-led government has so far supported Naidu 's extravagant Amaravati , despite two government-backed reports questioning many of the city 's aspects and Andhra Pradesh itself mired in debt . <p> In @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Andhra Pradesh Reorganisation Act 2014 , headed by K.C. Sivaramakrishnan and comprising urban planners and design experts , to explore site options for the new capital city . In August , the committee recommended that the Andhra Pradesh government focus on long-term development through building a larger landscape of cities and towns , rather than building a single ' super-city ' . It also said the large-scale takeover of fertile agricultural land was unfavourable , as this would lead to a lack of food security in the long run . <p> In December , the Andhra Pradesh Capital Region Development Authority Act 2014 or APCRDA established a group by the same name . The Andhra Pradesh Capital Region Development Authority was the body authorised to conduct the land pooling and build the city . The APCRDA commissioned Tata Consulting Engineers to issue an environmental impact assessment ( EIA ) report . When published , the report included a number of directives for the state government , including that the construction and subsequent functioning of Amaravati would not cause any environmental damage . <p> Despite these recommendations , in September , Naidu @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Mangalagiri -- the ' food bowl ' of the southern Krishna floodplains , one of the most fertile agrarian strips in the country -- would be the location of the state 's new capital city . <p> Khosla , Soni and Bolisetti contend that apart from being environmentally , economically and socially destructive , the Naidu government 's plans are in clear violation of the recommendations made by both the Sivaramakrishnan committee and the EIA . They claim that their Amaravati Natural City is an alternative to Naidu 's Subarna Jurong Amaravati , and also a model for a new kind of Indian city as the country continues to face water scarcity and climate change -- a city that demonstrates how humans can live healthily and harmoniously with nature . <p> Playing with water <p> The stretch along the Krishna on which Amaravati will be built is a highly fertile floodplain -- or a catchment area that replenishes itself naturally during rainfall and flooding , maintaining the water level of the soil , as well as the flow and ecology of the river , by continuously absorbing and discharging water @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ system of absorption and discharge and severely raise the risk of flash flooding , Khosla and Bolisetti explain . Several recent disasters have been the result of encroachment -- buildings , roads , dams and embankments -- on floodplains : the flooding of the Adyar and Coovam rivers in Chennai , of the Mithi river channel in Mumbai , in Srinagar and in Uttarakhand . Building Amaravati on the Krishna floodplain is akin to asking for a repeat of one of these disasters . <p> However , rather than being a threat to human life , floodplains could in fact be a resource -- as long as they are allowed to replenish and balance themselves . Soni explains that floodplain water is one of the last unpolluted sources of water for Indian cities : the water extracted from a floodplain is pure , since it comes from the late monsoon flood , which annually flushes out the pollution in the river . <p> Instead of destroying the floodplain with buildings , as the government-backed Subarna Jurong would do , Khosla , Soni and Bolisetti 's version of Amravati has planned to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ side of the Krishna . The preserved floodplain will easily yield an annual 60-75 million cubic metres ( MCM ) of water , which will be adequate for a population of a million people . The value of this yield at today 's prices can be taken as Rs 900 crores a year , in itself a great economic benefit . <p> Floodplains are shown either side of Krishna River . Credit : EIA Report <p> Delhi is already using this non-invasive method to obtain 56 MCM of water a year , drawn from a 20 km length of the northern Yamuna floodplains . The Delhi Jal Board , under chairman and water minister Kapil Mishra , plans to increase this supply to 100 MCM a year . <p> In a booklet on Amaravati Natural City , published in both Telegu and English and distributed to farmers , Khosla and Soni write of the severe and numerous water scarcity problems that India faces : polluted and overdrawn rivers , groundwater depletion and invasive dams . <p> The International Water Management Institute has categorised India as a ' water stress zone ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or moderately polluted across their entirety . India and China are the world 's largest countries with water stress , which will only become an irreversible catastrophe unless careful action is taken to conserve and manage water . <p> Khosla and his colleagues contend that urban planning in India needs to confront and work with the realities of water scarcity . Projects based on the ' McKinsey model ' -- Amaravati and others undertaken and planned by the BJP government -- do just the opposite . <p> Naidu 's government has been presenting Amaravati as a capital city ' for all ' -- as India 's first urban centre of truly global standards . But farmers , activists and others have been questioning the government 's motives and methods , asking just who the new city will benefit . <p> In a presentation in Delhi in July 2016 , Khosla and Soni described how the land chosen for Amaravati is currently the source of a vibrant agro-economy that yields Rs 1000 crore per year , with complete linkages from farm to market and many participating women entrepreneurs . The soil here @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ villages grow three or more crops annually and more than 120 varieties in total . Even marginal farmers who own half an acre or less earn more than Rs 30,000 each month . The destruction of the Krishna floodplain for the construction of Amaravati would mean the destruction of this rich local economy and these livelihoods . <p> In December 2014 , the Andhra Pradesh government began its own unique process of ' land pooling ' to acquire the 30,000 acres required for Amaravati . Ever since it started , farmers and others have claimed that the terms on which the pooling is taking place are economically unfair for farmers and that the government 's methods in convincing farmers to give up their lands coercive . They have pointed out that the APCRDA is solely comprised of businessmen and that there is a total lack of transparency and representation of the real stakeholders in the decision-making . <p> Following the start of land pooling , Bolisetti , who was contesting for the position of MP from the Visakhapatnam Lok Sabha constituency at the time , wrote a letter to Naidu elaborating @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ planned was such a bad idea . <p> The National Alliance of People 's Movement ( NAPM ) , a network of social activists , has been spearheading the opposition since . In January 2015 , it alleged that the Naidu government has a " hidden agenda " . Former IAS officer M.G. Devasahayam , who led a fact-finding team of NAPM for investigating the 29 villages within the planned capital region , said that the APCRDA Act was " introduced in the Assembly overnight and passed with no public consultation " , and was " against the Constitution and ... violative of the Land Acquisition Law of 2013 " . <p> NAPM also organised a national convention in Delhi , in January 2015 , in collaboration with the All India Union of Forest Working People , Delhi Solidarity Group , Jan Adhikar Sangharsh Samiti and other groups , to oppose the BJP government 's Land Ordinance Bill , which withdrew certain requirements in land acquisition for certain projects , such as the Social Impact Assessment and consent of land owners . The Bill lapsed in August and never came into @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ question of the constitutionality of the APCRDA Act , which is based on the Bill , and the method of land acquisition it allows , remains . <p> These farmers and critics have been questioning how the Andhra Pradesh government can proceed with a project that affects lakhs of individuals without real participation from those very people , is clearly environmentally destructive and finally how the Union government can back it . <p> Amaravati is destructive and inequitable in its very conception and creation and promises to remain so after it is built . A city like Amaravati -- of glass , steel and concrete , posh centres and poor peripheries , flyovers connecting peripheries to the centre , malls and high-rise complexes -- is friendly only to those who want and can afford the consumerist lifestyle that will feed such a city and who can afford to avoid the waste and pollution that will result from it . <p> Moreover , as Khosla and Soni write , " Asia can not copy the industrialised countries which have stable cities , landscapes and populations . In Asia there is too much @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do generalise about the vast and varied region of Asia here , their insights make meaningful the fact that many of Asia 's cities , including Delhi and Mumbai in India , have become invasive , feeding their own cycles of poverty and pollution . The Subarna Jurong city promises to turn into another such invasive mega-city . It presents no sustainable or equitable alternatives . <p> Through their concept of the ' natural city ' , Khosla and his colleagues have been arguing that urban spaces in India need to be planned so as to be in balance , with themselves and their environments -- to be ' natural ' rather than ' invasive ' , in the sense that " all living organisms maintain a steady state reflected in their internal equilibrium " . In other words , India needs to fundamentally redefine and redesign its idea of urbanisation to cohesively address the problems of climate change , resource and energy scarcity , social inequity and poverty . <p> Seed capital boundary in red line which is on 100% on the floodplains . Credit : EIA Report <p> Some @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's internal equilibrium and cohesiveness in values and practice -- the simultaneous preservation and use of the Krishna floodplains and its waters is one such feature . <p> Another positive feature is its checkerboard layout . In Khosla and Soni 's blueprint , built spaces alternate with open spaces of farms , pastures , orchards and forests , each block is two kilometres squared . The urban farms , pastures and orchards , irrigated by the city 's treated wastewater , will make the city self-sufficient in vegetables , fruits and dairy products . And such a layout will ensure ' green convention ' -- pulling cooler air from the green spaces into the warmer built areas -- and bring down the overall temperature of the city by two or three degrees , just one of the many ways the city will be energy efficient and run on the principle of recycling rather than consumption and waste . <p> At the same time , the city 's checkerboard design will work towards diversity in jobs and social groups , as individuals in occupations traditionally considered ' rural ' or ' urban @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to entrepreneurial innovation and talent as new climate , water and landscape-related jobs are created . <p> In a report on the Modi government 's Smart Cities project , Khosla writes : <p> " ... the new Smart Cities are primarily the product of a global financial imagination where mobile capital can describe its own deals and then descend to feed off them before moving on ... A smart city is a giant corporation within which there are small-er corporations and within which there are still smaller sub-corporations all of whom have invested in the city and whose investments are at stake ...
